One rig to control them all
Chris Heunen, Robin Kaarsgaard, Louis Lemonnier

Abstract
We introduce a theory for computational control, consisting of eight naturally interpretable equations. Adding these to a prop of base circuits constructs controlled circuits, borne out in examples of reversible Boolean circuits and quantum circuits. We prove that this syntactic construction semantically corresponds to taking the free rig category on the base prop.



1
Introduction

Many computations contain
controlled
commands, that is, commands that are executed depending on the value of some memory cell.
By
control
we mean the aspects of a computation that govern these dependencies.
1
1

1

We do not mean ‘control theory’ as used in e.g. engineering
[
22
]
.

Typically, the controlled command acts on one part of memory, and the controlling memory cell resides in another part of memory.
To be more precise, for example, consider controlled negation in reversible Boolean circuits: the target bit is flipped depending on the value of the control bit.
The goal of this article is to identify, separate, and study in isolation, this notion of computational control.

Traditional control flow or data flow is often mixed up with other computational aspects of circuits.
For example, in reversible Boolean or quantum circuits, multi-controlled gates such as the Toffoli gate are integral to universality and not treated differently than other, uncontrolled, gates
[
43
]
.
Yet separating out the controlled aspects of a computation as specified by a circuit has several benefits.

1.

Multi-controlled gates are of foundational importance in many computational theories, including Boolean logic
[
45
]
, reversible computation
[
43
,
42
]
, and quantum computation
[
39
]
. Isolating their control logic can help to better
understand
these theories.

2.

In quantum hardware, (multi-)controlled gates are among the most costly ones to perform physically
[
5
,
47
,
13
]
. Separating out the control aspects can help find better optimisation strategies
[
4
]
.
In general, partitioning off control aspects can help to
optimise
computations in a generic way that is independent of the ‘base circuit theory’ and therefore more efficient to apply.

3.

Several recent results about logical completeness for quantum computation rely on elaborate families of equations
[
7
,
6
,
32
,
15
,
14
,
16
,
18
,
17
,
23
]
. Cordoning off control aspects can
simplify
, and thereby clarify the core status of some and make them more modular.

f

Figure 1:
Control equations.

This article addresses these three challenges by introducing a theory of control governed by a handful of equations (see
Figure

1
). We argue that these equations completely capture control as follows.

1.

The equations have clear computational interpretations, and several have appeared in the literature before
[
38
,
42
]
.
Additionally, we show that the equations are canonical in a strong way, by relating them to the natural mathematical notion of a
rig category

[
31
,
46
]
.
Starting with an arbitrary ‘base circuit theory’, we syntactically construct a new ‘controlled circuit theory’. We do this in the most general setting possible, using
prop
s
[
33
,
25
,
9
]
. The construction has a universal property: roughly, the controlled prop is the free rig category on the base prop.
This is borne out in examples: starting with the circuit theory consisting of a single NOT gate, the controlled theory is universal for reversible Boolean circuits. Starting with an additional Hadamard gate, the controlled theory is universal for quantum circuits
[
40
]
. Starting with a single
\sqrt{X}
gate in fact suffices for the latter
[
29
]
.

2.

The coherence theorems for rig categories
[
31
,
46
]
, and the fact that the control theory (of
Figure

1
) consists of only eight unquantified equations, give rise to many optimisation strategies.
For example, we will show that the control theory suffices to syntactically derive equations like the following Sleator-Weinfurter decomposition quickly, that were before only known to hold semantically via matrix calculations in special cases
[
41
,
11
]
:

\scalebox{0.90}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}

3.

The equations simplify related work. The first works on complete
equational theories for quantum circuits
[
16
,
14
,
15
]
contain a notion of

structural
equations, without a mathematical account of this
notion. The same holds for
[
7
,
6
,
32
]
. The closest work
to ours is a sister notion of ‘controlled
prop’
[
21
]
, which is rather seen as a property of
a category rather than a construction. Their definition is more general
than ours, because they do consider NOT gates and colour changes. In
their sense, our construction outputs a twice ‘controlled prop’, with
a distinguished involution to change between the two controls. They
also do not relate to the structure of rig categories, which makes
their definition of ‘controlled prop’ incomplete in that sense.
Similarly, template-based rewrite
rules
[
42
]
for control in the
literature, which are not complete in our sense.

This article fills that gap by showing that the structure of control is
exactly that of rig categories. Our work similarly structures and
elucidates a line of research on quantum programming languages taking
semantics in rig categories
[
12
,
26
,
10
,
11
,
23
]
. Finally, our results obviate work on
circumventing no-control theorems by restricting to specific gate
sets
[
8
]
.

These results also substantiate the claim in the title, that rig structure encapsulates controlled computation, and only controlled computation. Thus rig categories form the bare minimum model of computation: the ability to compose instructions sequentially (with
\circ
), to consider data in parallel (with
\otimes
), and to use one piece of data to condition computations on another (using
\oplus
). This may also explain the ubiquity of matrices.

We proceed as follows. First, we reviews background material on props, rig categories, permutations, and Gray codes (in
Section

2
).
Then we introduce the main syntactic construction (in
Section

3
), and show that it semantically corresponds to rig completion (in
Section

4
). The latter proof uses in a pleasingly structural way the new technique of
Gray induction
, that proves a property for bitstrings by induction not on the normal successor of natural numbers, but by its Gray code.
After that, we discuss several applications of the theory (in
Section

5
): a singly-generated universal theory for controlled reversible Boolean circuits, a generalised Sleator-Weinfurter decomposition
[
41
]
, a relationship to modal quantum theory
[
37
]
, and a universal singly-generated theory for controlled quantum circuits
[
29
]
, and complete equational theories for quantum circuits over various gate sets.
We conclude by indicating possible future work (in
Section

6
).

2
Background

2.1
Props, rig categories, and bipermutative categories

We assume familiarity with basic category theory, including (strict)
symmetric monoidal categories, and recall here some basic facts about
props, rig categories, and bipermutative categories. For a more in
depth treatment on these topics, the interested reader is invited to
consult, e.g.,
[
46
,
33
]
.

A
prop
is a strict symmetric monoidal category whose objects
are natural numbers, and where the monoidal product is given by
addition. A
prop morphism
is an identity-on-objects strict
symmetric monoidal functor between props. Props are naturally regarded
as categories of diagrams, and play a similar role in the presentation
of symmetric monoidal categories as free groups do in combinatorial
group theory (see, e.g.,
[
9
]
).

Rig categories are categories that are symmetric monoidal in two
different ways, such that one distributes over the other, similar to
how multiplication distributes over addition in arithmetic. More
formally, a rig category
(\mathbf{C},\otimes,\oplus,O,I)
has two
symmetric monoidal structures
(\mathbf{C},\otimes,I)
(the

(tensor) product
, with associator
\alpha^{\otimes}
, unitors

\lambda^{\otimes}
and
\rho^{\otimes}
, and symmetry
\gamma^{\otimes}
)
and
(\mathbf{C},\oplus,O)
(the
(direct) sum
, with associator

\alpha^{\oplus}
, unitors
\lambda^{\oplus}
and
\rho^{\oplus}
, and
symmetry
\gamma^{\oplus}
), and come with natural coherence
isomorphisms witnessing distributivity on the right
\delta_{R}:(A\oplus B)\otimes C\to(A\otimes C)\oplus(B\otimes C)
and

\delta^{0}_{R}:O\otimes A\cong O
and analogously on the left,
subject to a significant number of coherence conditions (see

[
46
]
). When it is clear from the context, we will
elide marking coherence isomorphisms with their monoidal structure,
and simply write, e.g.,
\alpha
rather than
\alpha^{\oplus}
.

Bipermutative categories are the strictified version of rig
categories: the two symmetric monoidal structures are strict, and
right distributivity becomes an equality (such that
(A\oplus B)\otimes C=(A\otimes C)\oplus(B\otimes C)
), as do both nullary
distributors (such that
A\otimes O=O=O\otimes A
), subject to
three coherence conditions (see
[
34
,
46
]
; the full
definition of bipermutative category is also given in
Appendix
A
). That this is the right notion of strict rig
category is shown by the coherence theorem that every rig category is
rig equivalent to a bipermutative one
[
34
, Prop. 3.5]
. We say
that a bipermutative category is
semisimple
when its object are
natural numbers, and the two strict symmetric monoidal structures are
multiplication and addition of natural numbers on objects. Semisimple
bipermutative categories are naturally regarded as props by forgetting
the tensor product
(\otimes,I)
.

Proposition 1
.

Given a semisimple bipermutative category
\mathbf{C}
and a natural number

k
, the full subcategory of
\mathbf{C}
whose objects are powers of
k
is a
prop
(\mathbf{C}_{k},\otimes,1)
.

Remark 2
.

By using the proposition above to work on bits by choosing powers of
2
,
we have a natural involution with the symmetry
\gamma_{1,1}
. If all
morphisms are endomorphisms, then we obtain a controllable prop
(\mathbf{C}_{2},\otimes,1,\gamma_{1,1})
as defined below.

We provide the most natural example of such a category in the next section.

2.2
Permutations

An important example of a semisimple bipermutative category is the
category of permutations
\mathbf{Perm}
: its objects are natural
numbers, and morphisms
n\to n
are permutations
[n]\to[n]
. The

direct sum

\oplus
, on objects, simply sums the numbers, and
on morphisms, concatenates them.

f\oplus g=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccll}[m+n]&\to&[m+n]&\\
i&\mapsto&f(i)&\text{if }i<m\text{,}\\
i&\mapsto&g(i-m)+m&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

The symmetry
\gamma
for
\oplus
is defined as follows.

\gamma_{m,n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccll}[m+n]&\to&[n+m]&\\
i&\mapsto&n+i&\text{if }i<m\text{,}\\
i&\mapsto&i-m&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

The
tensor product

\otimes
multiplies the two numbers on objects, and acts on morphisms as follows.

f\otimes g=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccll}[mn]&\to&[mn]&\\
an+b&\mapsto&f(a)n+g(b)&\end{array}\right.

The symmetry
s
for
\otimes
is defined as:

s_{m,n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccll}[mn]&\to&[nm]&\\
an+b&\mapsto&bm+a&\\
\end{array}\right.

In fact,
\mathbf{Perm}
is both the free prop and the free bipermutative
category. This means that the tensor product and its symmetries can
entirely be described in terms of
\oplus
and
\gamma^{\oplus}
. In
particular, for all
f\colon m\to m
and
g\colon n\to n
:

\displaystyle f\otimes g

\displaystyle=s_{n,m}\circ(\underbrace{f\oplus f\oplus\cdots\oplus f}_{n\text{ times}})\circ s_{m,n}\circ(\underbrace{g\oplus g\oplus\cdots\oplus g}_{m\text{ times}})

(1)

\displaystyle=(\underbrace{g\oplus g\oplus\cdots\oplus g}_{m\text{ times}})\circ s_{n,m}\circ(\underbrace{f\oplus f\oplus\cdots\oplus f}_{n\text{ times}})\circ s_{m,n}

(2)

Note that permutations are generated by transpositions

\tau_{i}=(i\;\;i+1)
, subject to equations
\tau_{i}^{2}=\mathrm{id}
and
(\tau_{i}\tau_{i+1})^{3}=\mathrm{id}
and
\tau_{i}\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}\tau_{i}
when
i+1<j
.

2.3
Gray codes

In this paper, we work with a similar but different way of generating
permutations with transpositions on Gray codes. Gray codes are a way
of ordering bit strings such that the transition from any bit string to its successor consists of a single bit flip–i.e., such that the Hamming distance between adjacent bit strings is
1
. Gray codes play an
important role in the synthesis of reversible circuits (see, e.g.,

[
35
]
).

\begin{array}[]{c|c}\text{Binary}&\text{Gray}\\
\hline\cr 000&000\\
001&001\\
010&011\\
011&010\\
100&110\\
101&111\\
110&101\\
111&100\end{array}

Figure 2:
A Gray code on bit strings of length
3
.

For example, a Gray code of bit strings of length
3
is shown in
Figure
2
.

More formally, we define a function
h_{n}\colon[2^{n}]\to\{0,1\}^{n}
as:

h_{0}(0)=\varepsilon\qquad h_{n}(i)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0\cdot h_{n-1}(i)&\text{if }i<2^{n-1}\text{,}\\
1\cdot h_{n-1}(2^{n}-1-i)&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

We then fix
Gray transpositions

\theta_{n,i}
to swap

h_{n}(i)
with
h_{n}(i+1)
. The two latter words have common prefix
w_{n}(i)

and common suffix
w^{\prime}_{n}(i)
.
We also define permutations
r_{n}\colon[2^{n}]\to[2^{n}]
that translate
between the lexicographic order and the Gray order:

r_{0}(0)=\mathrm{id}_{1}\qquad r_{n}(i)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r_{n-1}(i)&\text{if }i<2^{n-1}\text{,}\\
2^{n-1}+r_{n-1}(2^{n}-1-i)&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

In other words, the transposition
\theta_{n,i}
swaps
r_{n}(i)
and
r_{n}(i+1)
.

Proposition 3
.

For all
n
and
i
, we have:

\theta_{n,i}=r_{n}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{i}\oplus\gamma_{1,1}\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}-2-i})\circ r_{n}\text{.}

Since the translation permutations are defined by induction on the number of
(qu)bits, we then obtain an inductive definition for the Gray transpositions.

\theta_{n+1,i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\theta_{n,i}\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}&\text{if }i<2^{n}-1\text{,}\\
s_{2^{n},2}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus\gamma_{1,1}\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}-2})\circ s_{2,2^{n}}&\text{if }i=2^{n}-1\text{,}\\
\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus\theta_{n,2^{n+1}-1-i}&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

(3)

3
Controlled props

In this section, we will show how to freely adjoin control to a prop,
such that arbitrarily controlled versions of all morphisms of the
original prop are morphisms in the controlled prop. For this
to make sense, the prop must satisfy some lightweight requirements,
namely those of a
controllable prop
.

Definition 4
.

A
controllable prop

(\mathbf{P},+,0,x)
, or
crop
for short, is
a prop
(\mathbf{P},+,0)
whose morphisms are endomorphisms and in which one
morphism
x\colon 1\to 1
is a distinguished involution. We sometimes
refer to this involution as the
NOT gate
. A crop morphism is a prop
morphism of the underlying props that preserves the chosen involution.

One important example of controllable prop is the prop
\mathbf{X}
generated
only by the NOT gate
x
.

We introduce a construction on controllable props, that consists in adding a
control operator. In order to have readable equations and better presentation,
we allow ourselves to write both a
positive
control (on the left) and a

negative
control (on the right). In fact, only one of them is necessary,
since the other can be obtained by conjugating with the NOT morphism
(see
4
in Definition
5
).

Both these operators are defined as functors. This means that they preserve
both composition and identities.

We represent the chosen involution
x\colon 1\to 1
with the usual NOT gate.

Because props are also equipped with a symmetry for the tensor, we can also
obtain controlled operation with a control wire at the bottom.

In the rest of the paper, we draw a single wire to picture any object
n
.

Definition 5

(Controlled prop)
.

Given a controllable prop
(\mathbf{P},+,0,x)
, we extend it to a new
prop with endofunctors
C^{0}
and
C^{1}
such that if
f\colon n\to n
,
then
C^{b}(f)\colon 1+n\to 1+n
, and that, for all
n
and
f,f_{1},f_{2}\colon n\to n
, we have equations:

1.

composition:
C^{1}(g\circ f)=C^{1}(g)\circ C^{1}(f)
;

2.

identity:
C^{1}(\mathrm{id}_{n})=\mathrm{id}_{n+1}
;

3.

strength:
C^{1}(f+\mathrm{id}_{m})=C^{1}(f)+\mathrm{id}_{m}
;

4.

colour change:
(x+\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ C^{0}(f)\circ(x+\mathrm{id}_{n})=C^{1}(f)
;

5.

complementarity:
C^{0}(f)\circ C^{1}(f)=\mathrm{id}_{1}+f
;

6.

commutativity:
C^{0}(f_{1})\circ C^{1}(f_{2})=C^{1}(f_{2})\circ C^{0}(f_{1})
;

7.

“swap”:

C^{1}(x)\circ\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\circ C^{1}(x)\circ\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\circ C^{1}(x)=\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}
;

8.

“swap” coherence:
(\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ C^{1}(C^{1}(f))=C^{1}(C^{1}(f))\circ(\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})
.

Figure
1
gives a diagrammatic account of
the above equations. Write
\mathbf{cP}
for this new prop, that we call
the
controlled prop
of
\mathbf{P}
. Note that
(\mathbf{cP},+,0,x)
is
itself a controllable prop.

Remark 6
.

The strength and composition equations above imply that, for all
f\colon m\to m
and
g\colon n\to n
, we have:

}}\colon(\mathrm{id}_{2}+\mathsf{swap}_{1,1})\circ C^{1}(C^{0}(C^{0}(x)))\circ(\mathrm{id}_{2}+\mathsf{swap}_{1,1})\text{,}

that we denote by
C^{10}_{0}(x)
for readability, and which are well-defined
thanks to Equation
8
. More generally, given two Boolean words

w
and
w^{\prime}
, we write
C^{w}_{w^{\prime}}(f)
for the gate
f
controlled on
\left|w\right|

top wires, positively or negatively according to
w
, and controlled on

\left|w^{\prime}\right|
bottom wires, according to
w^{\prime}
.

Proposition 7
.

A controllable prop
\mathbf{P}
is isomorphic to the wide subcategory of

\mathbf{cP}
whose morphisms are only the non-controlled ones. This also means
that there is an embedding
\mathbf{P}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{cP}
.

There are two forms of multiple controlled morphisms. Already in
\mathbf{c\mathbf{P}}
, one can control morphisms from
\mathbf{P}
on multiple wires. In
\mathbf{c\mathbf{c\mathbf{P}}}
, one can additionally formally control morphisms from
\mathbf{c\mathbf{P}}
.
In fact, the assignment
\mathbf{c}-
is a monad on the category of crops and crop morphisms.
The monad multiplication flattens the latter type of multiple control, but because the two forms of multiple control are distinct, the monad is not idempotent.
The monad unit is the embedding from the previous proposition.

Relation to permutations.

The simplest example of a controlled prop is already quite meaningful, as it captures
all classical computing. To justify this statement, we prove that the controlled
prop
\mathbf{cX}
of the prop
\mathbf{X}
generated by a single involution is
isomorphic to the category of permutations between finite sets of cardinality a power of
2
.

Theorem 8
.

The controlled prop
\mathbf{cX}
is crop isomorphic to
\mathbf{Perm}_{2}
.

To prove this, we define functors in both directions and show that they are
inverse of each other. Let us first define a crop morphism
\alpha\colon\mathbf{cX}\to\mathbf{Perm}_{2}
.

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\alpha\colon&\mathbf{cX}&\to&\mathbf{Perm}_{2}\\
&n&\mapsto&2^{n}\\
&x&\mapsto&\gamma_{1,1}\\
&\mathsf{swap}_{m,n}&\mapsto&s_{2^{m},2^{n}}\\
&C^{1}(f)&\mapsto&\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus\alpha(f)\\
&C^{0}(f)&\mapsto&\alpha(f)\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\\
&\mathrm{id}_{m}+f&\mapsto&\mathrm{id}_{2^{m}}\otimes\alpha(f)\end{array}

Lemma 9
.

The crop morphism
\alpha\colon\mathbf{cX}\to\mathbf{Perm}_{2}
is well-defined.

The proof consists in showing that any equation that holds in
\mathbf{cX}
also
holds in
\mathbf{Perm}_{2}
after the application of
\alpha
. In summary,
the equations
1
and
2
hold in

\mathbf{Perm}_{2}
because
\oplus
is a (bi)functor;
3
follows
by distributivity coherence;
4
by naturality of the symmetry

\gamma
for
\oplus
;
5
and

6
by bifunctoriality of
\oplus
;
7
by
naturality of
\gamma
and associative coherence.

We now define a functor
\beta\colon\mathbf{Perm}_{2}\to\mathbf{cX}
which maps a
Gray code transposition
\theta_{n,i}
to its associated multi-controlled NOT:

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\beta\colon&\mathbf{Perm}_{2}&\to&\mathbf{cX}\\
&2^{n}&\mapsto&n\\
&\theta_{n,i}&\mapsto&C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(x)\end{array}

where
w_{n}(i)
and
w^{\prime}_{n}(i)
are respectively the common prefix and suffix of
the
i^{\text{th}}
word in the Gray code
h_{n}(i)
. In a Gray code order, two
successive Boolean words have only one bit that differs, for example
1011
and

1001
when
n=4
and
i=13
. The operation that permutes these two words is a
NOT gate on the third bit, triggered when the first bit is
1
, the second
0

and the fourth
1
. This particular example corresponds to the multi-controlled
gate
C^{10}_{1}(x)
in
\mathbf{cX}
:

We use the induction formula for Gray code transpositions (
3
)
and the fact that all permutations are generated by (Gray code) transpositions
to prove that
\beta\colon\mathbf{Perm}_{2}\to\mathbf{cX}
is a well-defined
functor, and is in fact a crop morphism.

Lemma 10
.

The functor
\beta\colon\mathbf{Perm}_{2}\to\mathbf{cX}
is a well-defined crop
morphism, and

\beta(\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus f)=C^{1}(\beta(f))\text{,}\qquad\beta(f\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}})=C^{0}(\beta(f))\text{,}

for all morphisms
f\colon 2^{n}\to 2^{n}
.

Proof.

We recall that any permutation
2^{n}\to 2^{n}
is generated by Gray code
transpositions
\theta_{n,i}
, with the equations below, respectively
referred to as involution, Yang-Baxter
[
36
]
, and
commutativity.

\forall i<2^{n}-1,\theta_{n,i}^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\text{,}\qquad\forall i<2^{n}-2,(\theta_{n,i}\theta_{n,i+1})^{3}=\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\text{,}\qquad\forall i+1<j,\theta_{n,i}\theta_{n,j}=\theta_{n,j}\theta_{n,i}\text{.}

The functor
\beta\colon\mathbf{Perm}_{2}\to\mathbf{cX}
is well-defined: the
involution equation follows from
1
and

2
; Yang-Baxter is proven by induction, the base case on
two (qu)bits is a direct consequence of
7
, alongside

4
and
5
, and the rest follows
thanks to the induction formula for Gray codes and
8
; the
commutation is an easy consequence of
6
in
Definition
5
.

This functor is monoidal: first note that in
\mathbf{Perm}_{2}
, we have

s_{2,2}=\theta_{2,1}\circ\theta_{2,2}\circ\theta_{2,1}
(and we have

h_{2}(1)=01
,
h_{2}(2)=11
and
h_{2}(3)=10
), whose image through
\beta
is then:

\displaystyle\beta(\theta_{2,1}\circ\theta_{2,2}\circ\theta_{2,1})

\displaystyle=\beta(\theta_{2,1})\circ\beta(\theta_{2,2})\circ\beta(\theta_{2,1})

\displaystyle=C_{1}(x)\circ C^{1}(x)\circ C_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(def)}}}{{=}}\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\circ C^{1}(x)\circ\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\circ C^{1}(x)\circ\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\circ C^{1}(x)\circ\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{cprop:swap}}}{{=}}\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\circ\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}\circ\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}=\mathsf{swap}_{1,1}

thus
\beta
preserves the symmetry of the tensor; additionally, we know that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}_{2}\otimes\theta_{n,i}

\displaystyle=\theta_{n,i}\oplus\theta_{n,i}

\displaystyle=(\theta_{n,i}\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus\theta_{n,i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:ind-theta})}}{{=}}\theta_{n+1,i}\circ\theta_{n+1,2^{n+1}-1-i}

and therefore, since
i<2^{n}-2
, we have:

\displaystyle\beta(\mathrm{id}_{2}\otimes\theta_{n,i})

\displaystyle=\beta(\theta_{n+1,i})\circ\beta(\theta_{n+1,2^{n+1}-1-i})

\displaystyle=C^{w_{n+1}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n+1}(i)}(x)\circ C^{w_{n+1}(2^{n+1}-1-i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n+1}(2^{n+1}-1-i)}(x)

\displaystyle=C^{0}\left(C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(x)\right)\circ C^{1}\left(C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(x)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{cprop:complementarity}}}{{=}}\mathrm{id}_{1}+C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(x)=\mathrm{id}_{1}+\beta(\theta_{n,i})

which concludes that the functor
\beta
is monoidal, since any
permutations is generated by the
\theta_{n,i}
. Moreover, since we have

\theta_{1,0}=\gamma_{1,1}
, and since its image by
\beta
is
x
, the
functor
\beta
preserves the NOT gate. Therefore,
\beta
is a crop
morphism.

Additionally, we prove that for all
f
, we have
\beta(\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus f)=C^{1}(\beta(f))
. Once again, it suffices to prove it for the
generators, therefore:

\displaystyle\beta(\mathrm{id}\oplus\theta_{n,i})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:ind-theta})}}{{=}}\beta(\theta_{n+1,2^{n+1}-1-i})

\displaystyle=C^{w_{n+1}(2^{n+1}-1-i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n+1}(2^{n+1}-1-i)}(x)

\displaystyle=C^{1}\left(C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=C^{1}(\beta(f))

and similarly, we have
\beta(f\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}})=C^{0}(\beta(f))
.
∎

This implies that
\beta
is the inverse of
\alpha
, which
suffices to prove Theorem
8
.

4
Rigged props thanks to Kronecker

As shown in the previous section, in particular in the definition of the
functor
\alpha
, control is linked to the additive tensor
\oplus
, which we
loosely call
direct sum
. This means that if we can extend a ‘base
circuit theory’ with direct sums, we should get its controlled
circuit theory. In fact, the better way is to start from a prop generated by
the direct sum, and then recapture the ‘base circuit theory’ on the tensor
product
\otimes
from there.

In the context of matrices, the Kronecker product can be computed with direct
sums only. Given a matrix
M
, the matrix
I_{n}\otimes M
is obtained as the
block-diagonal matrix

\left.\begin{bmatrix}M&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&M\end{bmatrix}\right\}n\text{ times}\text{,}

or more precisely,
M\oplus\dots\oplus M
. Additionally, the
symmetry for the tensor product
\otimes
can be expressed in terms of composition
of symmetries for the direct sum. This shows that any theory around the direct
sum can simulate the one of a tensor product, given some coherence. One of
these is the bifunctorality of the tensor product, or in pictures, the
following equality:

}}

In a bipermutative category, with
f\colon k\to l
and
g\colon m\to n
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{lll}f\otimes g&=(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes g)&=(\mathrm{id}_{l}\otimes g)\circ(f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{m})\\
&=s_{n,l}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{n}\otimes f)\circ s_{k,n}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{k}\otimes g)&=(\mathrm{id}_{l}\otimes g)\circ s_{m,l}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{m}\otimes f)\circ s_{k,m}\\
&=s_{n,l}\circ(\underbrace{f\oplus\dots\oplus f}_{n\text{
times}})\circ s_{k,n}\circ(\underbrace{g\oplus\dots\oplus g}_{k\text{ times}})&=(\underbrace{g\oplus\dots\oplus g}_{l\text{ times}})\circ s_{m,l}\circ(\underbrace{f\oplus\dots\oplus f}_{m\text{ times}})\circ s_{k,m}\\
\end{array}

To reflect this in a tensor product
\otimes
generated by a direct sum

\oplus
, the last line above needs to be enforced, leading to our definition of
rigged prop below.

4.1
Rigged props

We are now able to define rigged props as described above: a new prop whose
monoidal structure is meant as a direct sum. Its generators are the ones from
the base prop, for which we adjust the domains and codomains to a matrix point
of view. The relations for this new prop are the ones of the base prop, in addition to the relations (
5
) that ensure that the newly obtained
tensor product is bifunctorial.

Definition 11

(Rigged prop)
.

Given a prop
(\mathbf{P},+,0)
with a set of generators
G
, and a set of
relations
R
, its
rigged prop

(\mathbf{\widetilde{P}},\oplus,0)
is the prop
generated by

\widetilde{G}=\left\{\widetilde{g}\colon 2^{k}\to 2^{l}~\mid~g\colon k\to l\in G\right\}

(4)

with equations

\displaystyle\widetilde{R}=~

\displaystyle\left\{\widetilde{f+\mathrm{id}}\circ\widetilde{\mathrm{id}+h}=\widetilde{\mathrm{id}+h}\circ\widetilde{f+\mathrm{id}}~\mid~f,h\in G\right\}

(5)

\displaystyle\cup\left\{\widetilde{f}=\widetilde{h}~\mid~(f=h)\in R\right\}

(6)

where
\widetilde{\cdot}
is defined as follows for
f\colon k\to l
,
g\colon l\to m
,
and
h\colon m\to n
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{id}_{n}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}

(7)

\displaystyle\widetilde{g\circ f}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{g}\circ\widetilde{f}

(8)

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}\otimes\widetilde{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{{\scriptsize def}}}}{{=}}\widetilde{f+h}

\displaystyle=s_{2^{n},2^{l}}\circ(\underbrace{\widetilde{f}\oplus\widetilde{f}\oplus\cdots\oplus\widetilde{f}}_{2^{n}\text{
times}})\circ s_{2^{k},2^{n}}\circ(\underbrace{\widetilde{h}\oplus\widetilde{h}\oplus\cdots\oplus\widetilde{h}}_{2^{k}\text{ times}})

(9)

Relations (
5
) and (
6
) ensure that
\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}

is larger than
\mathbf{P}
, meaning that there is an embedding
\mathbf{P}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}
.

Theorem 12
.

Given a prop
\mathbf{P}
, its rigged prop
\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}
is semisimple
bipermutative.

Proof.

There is a prop morphism
E\colon\mathbf{Perm}\to\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}
since

\mathbf{Perm}
is the free prop. The
\otimes
monoidal structure on
\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}
can then be defined the same way as in
\mathbf{Perm}
. The tensor

\otimes
in
\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}
is indeed a bifunctor, mainly thanks to
(
5
). Equation (
9
) ensures that the
symmetry of the tensor
\oplus
is natural. The coherence equations are
inherited from
\mathbf{Perm}
through
E
. Therefore,
\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}
is
semisimple bipermutative.
∎

This construction
\mathbf{\widetilde{\cdot}}
provides not merely a semisimple bipermutative
category, but the universal one.

Theorem 13
.

Given a prop
\mathbf{P}
and a strict monoidal functor to a semisimple
bipermutative category
F\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
such that
F(1)=2
,
there is a unique strict bipermutative functor
\widetilde{F}\colon\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}\to\mathbf{Q}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}}

Proof.

Let us first define the resulting functor. Since
F(1)=2
, we have

F(n)=2^{n}
. Thus we can define:

\begin{array}[]{c}\widetilde{F}(n)=n\qquad\widetilde{F}(\widetilde{g})=F(g)\end{array}

and this suffices to define the functor
\widetilde{F}
. All the equations
in
\widetilde{P}
derive from
P
or from the rig structure, and therefore

\widetilde{F}
is well-defined because it is strict rig and because
F
is
well-defined.
A strict rig functor between two semisimple bipermutative categories is
necessarily the identity on objects. Since the functor is obtained by the image
of
F
, it is necessarily unique.
∎

The domains and codomains of the generators for
\mathbf{\widetilde{P}}
, in (
4
),
can in fact be the power of any arbitrary natural number. We choose
2
to fit
the story of circuits on bits. Moreover, the definition of rigged prop does not
account for the NOT gate, which plays an central role in our theory of control.
This is the focus of the next section.

4.2
Rigged crops

We now present a definition of rigged props that takes into account the choice
of NOT gate. Whether it is classical reversible circuits or quantum circuits,
the NOT gate is represented as the following matrix:

\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}

which is also the symmetry
\gamma_{1,1}
for the direct sum
\oplus
. Given a
crop with a choice of NOT gate, we further identify the chosen NOT gate with
the symmetry
\gamma_{1,1}
in its constructed rigged prop, to obtain its
rigged crop.

Definition 14

(Rigged crop)
.

Given a controllable prop
(\mathbf{P},+,0,x)
with a set of generators
G
,
and a set of relations
R
, its
rigged crop

(\mathbf{\overline{P}},\oplus,0)

is the prop generated by

\overline{G}=\widetilde{G}

subject to equations

\overline{R}=\left\{\widetilde{x}=\gamma_{1,1}\right\}\cup\widetilde{R}\text{.}

The rigged crop is semisimple bipermutative in the same way as the
rigged prop.

Theorem 15
.

The category
(\mathbf{\overline{P}},\otimes,1,\oplus,0)
is semisimple
bipermutative.

Furthermore, rigged crops enjoy a similar universality as rigged props.

Theorem 16
.

Given a controllable prop
(\mathbf{P},+,0,x)
, and a strict monoidal
functor
F\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
to a semisimple bipermutative category
with
F(x)=\gamma_{1,1}
, there is a unique strict rig functor
\overline{F}\colon\mathbf{\overline{P}}\to\mathbf{Q}
such that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\overline{P}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}}

In the following, we are interested in objects in
\mathbf{\overline{P}}
that are powers of

2
. Given how generators in
\mathbf{P}
are mapped into
\mathbf{\overline{P}}
, the embedding

\mathbf{P}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\overline{P}}
corestricts to an embedding
\mathbf{P}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
.

Corollary 17
.

Given a controllable prop
(\mathbf{P},+,0,x)
, and a strict monoidal
functor
F\colon\mathbf{P}\to\mathbf{Q}
to a semisimple bipermutative category
with
F(x)=\gamma_{1,1}
, there is a unique prop morphism
\overline{F}_{2}\colon\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}\to\mathbf{Q}_{2}
such that
\overline{F}_{2}
preserves the NOT gate and
the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{F}_{2}}

Relation to permutations.

Observe that, similarly to the controlled prop on a single involution, the
rigged crop on a single involution is the category of permutations.

Theorem 18
.

The category
\mathbf{\overline{X}}
is exactly the category of permutations.

Proof.

Permutations are generated by transpositions
\tau_{i}=(i\;\;i+1)
, which can
be mapped in
\mathbf{\overline{X}}
to the involutions
\mathrm{id}_{i}\oplus\gamma_{1,1}\oplus\mathrm{id}_{n-3-i}
.
The latter commute if they act on separate parts of the
object. Moreover, the Yang-Baxter equation
(\tau_{i}\;\;\tau_{i+1})^{3}=\mathrm{id}
follows from naturality of
\gamma
and associative coherence.
Thus the functor
\mathbf{Perm}\to\mathbf{\overline{X}}
is well-defined and the identity on objects.
Because it
maps coherence morphisms to coherence morphisms, it is in fact a strict
bipermutative functor. As
\mathbf{\overline{X}}
, just like
\mathbf{Perm}
, only
contains coherence morphisms, the functor is an isomorphism.
∎

We now redefine the functors
\alpha\colon\mathbf{cX}\to\mathbf{\overline{X}}_{2}
and
\beta\colon\mathbf{\overline{X}}_{2}\to\mathbf{cX}
to fit the topic at hand.

Remark 19
.

In principle, the notion of control makes sense for any prop
consisting only of endomorphisms. However, we focus here on
groupoids, since this is where controlled gates originated. In the
more general, irreversible setting, control certainly makes sense,
though more space-efficient gadgets such as multiplexers are typically
used instead.

4.3
Rig is control

In this section, we fix a controllable prop
(\mathbf{P},+,0,x)
. It is now
a matter of proving that
\mathbf{cP}
and
\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
are isomorphic. First let
us define a functor
\mathbf{cP}
to
\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}A\colon&\mathbf{cP}&\to&\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}\\
&n&\mapsto&2^{n}\\
&f\in\mathbf{cX}&\mapsto&\alpha(f)\\
&g\in G&\mapsto&\overline{g}\\
&C^{1}(f)&\mapsto&\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus A(f)\\
&C^{0}(f)&\mapsto&A(f)\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\\
&\mathrm{id}_{m}+f&\mapsto&\mathrm{id}_{2^{m}}\otimes A(f)\end{array}

Lemma 20
.

The prop morphism
A\colon\mathbf{cP}\to\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
is well-defined.

Proof.

We need to prove that all equations that hold in
\mathbf{cP}
also hold in

\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
after taking the image under
A
. The equations of monoidal
coherence hold because
\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
is a prop. We further observe that if
f

does not contain any control, then
A(f)=\widetilde{f}
; therefore, all
original equations from
\mathbf{P}
hold by (
6
).
Additionally, the equations
1
and

2
hold in
\mathbf{Perm}_{2}
because
\oplus
is a
(bi)functor;
3
follows by coherence of distributivity;

4
by naturality of the symmetry
\gamma
for
\oplus
;

5
and
6
by
bifunctoriality of
\oplus
; and
7
by naturality of
\gamma
and
associative coherence.
∎

Similarly, we can define a functor
\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}\to\mathbf{cP}
.

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}B\colon&\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}&\to&\mathbf{cP}\\
&2^{n}&\mapsto&n\\
&f\in\mathbf{\overline{X}}_{2}&\mapsto&\beta(f)\\
&\overline{g}\in\overline{G}&\mapsto&g\\
&\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus f&\mapsto&C^{1}(B(f))\\
&f\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}&\mapsto&C^{0}(B(f))\end{array}

Lemma 21
.

The functor
B\colon\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}\to\mathbf{cP}
is a well-defined prop morphism.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbf{\overline{P}}
is generated by
\overline{G}
and permutations

\gamma
. Therefore, morphisms are of the form:

\gamma_{0}\circ(\mathrm{id}\oplus\widetilde{g_{1}}\oplus\mathrm{id})\circ\gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\gamma_{n-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}\oplus\widetilde{g_{n}}\oplus\mathrm{id})\circ\gamma_{n}

where
\gamma_{i}
are short for compositions of symmetries for the direct
sum
\oplus
. Since the generator terms are surrounded by permutations, we
can write them as follows, without loss of generality:

\gamma_{0}\circ(\mathrm{id}\oplus\widetilde{g_{1}})\circ\gamma_{1}\circ\dots\circ\gamma_{n-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}\oplus\widetilde{g_{n}})\circ\gamma_{n}

If this is a morphism
2^{m}\to 2^{m}
in
\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
, then the terms containing
generators
\widetilde{g}\colon 2^{k}\to 2^{k}
are of the form:

\mathrm{id}_{2^{m}-2^{k}}\oplus\widetilde{g}=\mathrm{id}_{2^{m-1}}\oplus(\mathrm{id}_{2^{m-2}}\oplus(\dots\oplus(\mathrm{id}_{2^{k}}\oplus\widetilde{g})\dots))

Therefore the definition of
B
above defines an image of all morphism in

\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
. We now prove that
B
is well-defined. All coherence equations
hold because
\beta
is well-defined. The only equation specific to
\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
is (
5
), which is obviously preserved since
\otimes

is a monoidal structure, provided that
B
is indeed a prop morphism. We
thus need to prove that
B(f\otimes g)=B(f)+B(g)
for all
f
and
g
. We know, through
\beta
, that
B(s_{2^{m},2^{n}})=\mathsf{swap}_{m,n}
, so that it suffices to show that
B(\mathrm{id}_{2}\otimes f)=\mathrm{id}_{1}+B(f)
.

\displaystyle B(\mathrm{id}_{2}\otimes f)

\displaystyle=B(f\oplus f)

\displaystyle=B(f\oplus\mathrm{id}_{n})\circ B(\mathrm{id}_{n}\oplus f)

\displaystyle=C^{0}(B(f))\circ C^{1}(B(f))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{cprop:complementarity}}}{{=}}\mathrm{id}_{1}+B(f)

Thus
B
is a prop morphism.
∎

Both functors are well-defined and each other’s inverse, yielding an isomorphism of props.

Theorem 22
.

The props
\mathbf{cP}
and
\mathbf{\overline{P}}_{2}
are isomorphic.

Thus the control equations of Figure
1
are exactly the ones that turn a crop into a rigged crop. In this sense, we conclude they are structural: they govern exactly the rig structure.
A fortiori, Corollary
17
shows that the control equations are minimal: our definition of controlled prop (see Definition
5
) is universal among such structural categories.

5
Applications

To showcase the usefulness of the control equations of Figure
1
, this section discusses five applications drawn from circuit theory, both Boolean and quantum, that can be handled easily using our control theory.

5.1
Reversible Boolean circuits

expressing that two NOT gates that are controlled on the same wire but have different targets always commute. This may seem obvious, but usual proofs resort to truth tables. In contrast, the proof below is equational, and demonstrates how the control equations interact. First, observe the following.

(11)

5.2
Sleator-Weinfurter

The Sleator-Weinfurter construction is a general construction for
forming a doubly controlled
f^{2}
-gate using nothing but controlled

f
-gates and controlled NOT gates (see Figure
3
). This
property has led to the quantum synthesis of reversible circuits being
studied by means of the NCV gate set
\{NOT,CV,CNOT\}
(see, e.g.,

[
1
]
). Originally shown through linear
algebra
[
5
]
, the construction has a simple proof
in terms of the control equations.

Figure 3:
Sleator-Weinfurter identity through control equations.

5.3
Schumacher-Westmoreland: modal quantum theory

Starting from a theory of matrices, it is now easy to generate a circuit
equational theory with our control completion of circuit props. We give an
example of this on modal quantum theory
[
37
]
.
The latter is a toy theory for quantum mechanics where scalars are not complex
numbers but drawn from a finite field, for example the two-element field

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. Of course this cannot express all of quantum theory, but it
still retains key notions such as entanglement, mixed states, superdense coding
and teleportation
[
37
]
. This makes modal quantum
theory quite a rich toy model, which makes modal quantum circuits, acting on
mobits
(modal bits), an interesting case study.

As pointed out by Lafont
[
30
]
, reversible matrices on
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
have
two generators

X=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\qquad J=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}

governed by the following complete
[
30
, Theorem 6]
set of
matrix equations.

\displaystyle X^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{2}

(12)

\displaystyle J^{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{2}

(13)

\displaystyle JXJ

\displaystyle=X

(14)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus X)(X\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus X)

\displaystyle=(X\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus X)(X\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})

(15)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus X)(J\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus X)

\displaystyle=(X\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus J)(X\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})

(16)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus J)(X\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus J)

\displaystyle=(J\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})(\mathrm{id}_{1}\oplus J)(J\oplus\mathrm{id}_{1})

(17)

The first three equations are simple mobit equations, (
15
) is
Yang-Baxter, and (
16
) is the naturality of the symmetry. The last one is
not structural, and translates to the following circuit equation.

\scalebox{0.90}{{
\hbox to43.68pt{\vbox to22.54pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.83957pt\lower-13.43053pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{{}}{}{

(18)

Proposition 23
.

Equation (
18
) is independent of the control equations
of Figure
1
and equations (
12
),
(
13
), and (
14
).

Proof.

Interpret diagrams in
X
and
J
in permutation matrices, by mapping

X
to the usual NOT gate and
J
to the identity. All control equations
and (
12
), (
13
), and (
14
) hold directly.
However, equation (
18
) does not hold in this model: the
left-hand side is a swap, whereas the right-hand side is
the identity.
∎

We show that this independent equation (
18
) is the unique
one necessary for completeness in addition to the mobit equations and control
equations.

Theorem 24
.

The control equations of Figure
1
and equations
(
12
), (
13
), (
14
) and
(
18
) are complete for mobit circuits interpreted in

\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
.

The same proof strategy as before works. Define functors between the circuit
prop obtained with mobit equations as well as control equations, and the prop

\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{2}
. Observe that
\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
is a bipermutative
category, and thus
\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{2}
is a crop. Write
\mathbf{J}
for the crop generated by
x
and
j
such that
x^{2}=\mathrm{id}_{1}
,
j^{3}=\mathrm{id}_{1}
, and
jxj=x
. Write
\mathbf{cJ}_{/\simeq}
for the controlled prop out
of
\mathbf{J}
further quotiented by (
18
).

Similar to Gray code transpositions (see §
2.3
), we define Gray
code
J
’s to be:

\zeta_{n,i}=r_{n}^{-1}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{i}\oplus J\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}-2-i})\circ r_{n}\text{.}

for which we can also obtain an inductive formulation.

\zeta_{n+1,i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\zeta_{n,i}\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}&\text{if }i<2^{n}-1\text{,}\\
s_{2^{n},2}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus J\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}-2})\circ s_{2,2^{n}}&\text{if }i=2^{n}-1\text{,}\\
\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus\left(\theta_{n,2^{n+1}-1-i}\circ\zeta_{n,2^{n+1}-1-i}\circ\theta_{n,2^{n+1}-1-i}\right)&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

(19)

This allows us the define the following prop morphisms, one direction obtained
through the prop and control structure, and the other because morphisms in

\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
are generated by
X
and
J
, this time in Gray code:

\theta_{n,i}
and
\zeta_{n,i}
. In the definitions below, the order
<_{\ell}

describes the lexicographic order.

\begin{array}[]{lrcllrcll}A_{J}\colon&\mathbf{cJ}_{/\simeq}&\to&\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{2}&\qquad B_{J}\colon&\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{2}&\to&\mathbf{cJ}_{/\simeq}\\
&n&\mapsto&2^{n}&&2^{n}&\mapsto&n\\
&f\in\mathbf{cX}&\mapsto&\alpha(f)&&\theta_{n,i}&\mapsto&C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(x)\\
&j&\mapsto&J&&\zeta_{n,i}&\mapsto&C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(j)&\text{if }h_{n}(i)<_{\ell}h_{n}(i+1)\text{,}\\
&C^{1}(f)&\mapsto&\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus A_{J}(f)&&\zeta_{n,i}&\mapsto&C^{w_{n}(i)}_{w^{\prime}_{n}(i)}(j^{2})&\text{otherwise.}\\
&C^{0}(f)&\mapsto&A_{J}(f)\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}&\\
&\mathrm{id}_{m}+f&\mapsto&\mathrm{id}_{2^{m}}\otimes A_{J}(f)&&&&\end{array}

The functor
A_{J}
is obviously well-defined for the same reason as
Lemma
20
and because (
18
) reflects
(
17
). We then treat the functor
B_{J}
the same way as
\beta\colon\mathbf{Perm}_{2}\to\mathbf{cX}
.

Lemma 25
.

The functor
B_{J}\colon\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{2}\to\mathbf{cJ}_{/\simeq}
is a well-defined
crop morphism, and

B_{J}(\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}}\oplus f)=C^{1}(B_{J}(f))\text{,}\qquad B_{J}(f\oplus\mathrm{id}_{2^{n}})=C^{0}(B_{J}(f))\text{,}

for all morphisms
f\colon 2^{n}\to 2^{n}
.

Proof.

In order to show that
B_{J}
is well-defined, we need to prove that all
equations that hold in
\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{2}
also hold after image by
B_{J}
.
To do so, we work with the Lafont representation given earlier in the
section. The equations (
12
), (
13
), and
(
14
) are straightforward.
Equation (
15
) is the usual Yang-Baxter, therefore holds after
applying
B_{J}
for the same reason it does for
\beta
(see
Lemma
10
). Similarly to Yang-Baxter for
\beta
,
well-definedness at (
16
) follows by induction, where the base
case (on two mobits) is:

}}=

J

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{cprop:swap}+\ref{cprop:composition}}}{{=}}

J

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{cprop:composition}}}{{=}}

J^{2}

and the rest of the induction is direct because
\beta
is well-defined. A
similar reasoning applies to (
17
), however the base case of
the induction cannot be proven simply by the rules of
\mathbf{cJ}
, this is
why (
18
) is added.
Additionally, We use the same strategy than in Lemma
10
to
prove that
B_{J}
is a prop morphism and maps the direct sums to control.
∎

Because
A_{J}
and
B_{J}
are well-defined crop morphisms and have inverse
images, the categories
\mathbf{cJ}_{/\simeq}
and
\mathbf{GL}({\mathbb{Z}}_{2})_{2}
are crop
isomorphic.

5.4
Controlled-V

An active area of research in quantum computing deals with determining
the precise resources necessary for universal quantum computing to
emerge
[
27
,
44
,
28
]
. As we have seen, in the classical case,
universality emerges from the simplest possible theory of control,
namely the one generated by a crop with a single involution

NOT:1\to 1
. A natural question to ask is how this prop must be
extended to give a model of universal quantum computation.

Recent work
[
29
]
shows that a controlled-
V
gate,
where
V
is a
square root
of the NOT gate (i.e., satisfies
V\circ V=NOT
), suffices to perform universal quantum computing. This
has the surprising consequence that a model of universal quantum
computation can be generated from the theory of control for a single
operation of order
4
.

Theorem 26
.

Let
(\mathbf{V},+,0,V^{2})
be the crop given by the prop presented by a
single generator
V:1\to 1
and relation
V^{4}=\mathrm{id}
. Then there
exists a prop morphism
\mathbf{c\mathbf{V}}\to\mathbf{Unitary}
whose
image is universal for quantum computing.

Proof.

Send
V
to the usual
V
-gate
\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1+i&1-i\\
1-i&1+i\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, and its controlled variants to the usual
(iterated) controlled
V
-gate.
∎

While this gives a model of universal quantum computing, its
equational theory is not complete (i.e., the functor is not faithful);
this can be seen from the fact that the image of
V
could have been
chosen to be the
S
-gate, which is not universal for quantum
computing even given arbitrary control (it generates a theory of

monomial matrices
). The precise relations needed to pin down
the theory of controlled-
V
is an open question.

5.5
Quantum circuits

A complete equational theory for quantum circuits was an open question that was
solved only recently
[
16
]
with several later
improvements
[
15
,
14
,
21
]
. These papers already refer to some equations as

structural
. We show here that these equations are structural in a formal
and categorical sense: they are only about control, and follow directly from the
structure of a rig category.

Conjugation plays an important role in controlled quantum
circuits
[
21
]
, because of the following equation.

(25)

where (
25
) is the well-known Euler decomposition, in which

\beta_{0}
,
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
and
\beta_{4}
can be computed from
\alpha_{1}

and
\alpha_{2}
deterministically. We choose the crop
(\mathbf{Z},+,0,HZ(\pi)H)

and can now form its controlled prop
\mathbf{cZ}
. Interestingly, the prop
\mathbf{cZ}
is not immediately the prop of unitary operations: for a given
\alpha
, the
morphisms
C^{1}(\alpha)
and
Z(\alpha)
have the same semantics in unitaries
but are still different in
\mathbf{cZ}
. We need to quotient further with the
following equation:

\scalebox{0.90}{{
\hbox to36.57pt{\vbox to12.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.39616pt\lower-6.31734pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -34.45 0 L 14.76 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \hbox to0.0pt{
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-24.89616pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{10.66978pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

and we write

for this category. From this point, it is simple to

]
, and therefore

is

Theorem 27
.

Equations (
21
), (
22
), (
23
),
(
24
), (
25
) together with the control equations of
Figure
1
and equation (
26
),
are sound and complete for quantum circuits.

Proof.

The set of generators is universal, and it suffices to show that all the required equations follow from ours. The circuit equations from the
literature
[
21
]
contain similar equations to
(
21
), (
22
), (
23
),
(
24
), and (
25
), given that equation
(
26
) holds. Moreover, we have proven that controlled
conjugation (
20
) holds in our control theory. This is enough to
cover the set of equations for quantum circuits
[
21
]
.
∎

(30)

to obtain the category
\mathbf{c\Pi}_{/\simeq}
. Recall that the
Gaussian
Clifford+
T
gate set
[
2
]
consists of the gates
S
,

K
,
X
,
CX
, and
CCX
, where
K=\tfrac{1-i}{\sqrt{2}}H
, and
H

is the usual Hadamard gate. We then obtain the desired result:

Theorem 28
.

Equations (
27
), (
28
), (
29
),
together with the control equations of
Figure
1
and equation
(
30
), are sound and complete for Clifford circuits,
Clifford+
T
circuits with
\leq 2
qubits, and Gaussian Clifford+
T
circuits.

Proof.

Establishing the required completeness results in

[
11
]
only required
\omega^{8}=\mathrm{id}
(which follows
by (
27
)),
V^{2}=\gamma_{1,1}
(which follows by
(
28
) and that fact that
V^{2}
is the chosen
involution), and
SVS=VSV
where
S=\mathrm{id}\oplus\omega^{2}
(which
follow from (
29
) and (
30
)), as well as the
axioms of rig categories, which are implied by the control equations
of Figure
1
by Theorem
22
.
∎

6
Conclusion

Our results raise several interesting questions, that we leave to future work.

•

The control equations of
Figure

1
have natural interpretations that seem to indicate they are independent. But is this set of equations indeed minimal? Can we find models that satisfy all but one of the control equations?

•

Several circuit optimisations in the literature rely on auxiliary
wires in a circuit to enact controlled
operations
[
14
,
15
]
, and other
works also make use of ancillas to obtain complete equational theories
for CNOTs
[
20
]
and
Toffolis
[
19
]
. How do these relate to rigged
crops?

•

There is no quantum circuit implementation of a controlled unitary where the unitary is a black box input
[
3
]
. Yet there are many physical implementations bypassing the strict confines of this no-go theorem
[
24
,
8
]
. The latter rely on the ability to identify subspaces of qubits by adding auxiliary dimensions, harmonising with our results. Is there a version of the construction of
Definition

5
that allows for auxiliary wires?

•

We have isolated the control aspects from the rest of the computation. Can this be used to analyse causal dependencies within the data flow of the computation?

•

We have worked out examples adding computational control to several props such as a single
X
gate, a single
\sqrt{X}
gate, and an
X
gate together with a Hadamard gate. What does the controlled prop look like for other concrete base circuit theories?

•

The control equations of
Figure

1
implicitly assume only two possibilities on each wire: positive and negative control. Can we extend the theory from two-valued data like bits or qubits to data with multiple values like qutrits?

•

We have worked with bipermutative categories, where the fact that
\oplus
is symmetric corresponds to the fact
x^{2}=1
that the NOT gate is an involution. Are there interesting theories when we generalise to
\oplus
being merely braided?

•

Boolean functions are fundamental to bounded decision diagrams and SAT-solving. Do our results imply anything practically useful in those applications?

Appendix A
Definitions

Definition 29

(Bipermutative category)
.

A
bipermutative category

(\mathbf{C},\otimes,I,\oplus,O)
is a
category such that
(C,\otimes,I)
and
(C,\oplus,O)
are strict
symmetric monoidal categories, and the following conditions are satisfied:

•

for all objects
A
, we have
O\otimes A=O=A\otimes O
; for
all morphisms
f\colon A\to B
, we have
f\otimes\mathrm{id}_{O}=\mathrm{id}_{O}=\mathrm{id}_{O}\otimes f
;

•

for all objects
A,B,C
and morphisms
f,g,h
, we have that

(A\oplus B)\otimes C=(A\otimes C)\oplus(B\otimes C)

(31)

and

(f\oplus g)\otimes h=(f\otimes h)\oplus(g\otimes h)

(32)

and the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\oplus B)\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes C)\oplus(B\otimes C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\oplus A)\otimes C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes C)\oplus(A\otimes C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

(33)

•

if we define a natural left distributor
\delta
as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes(B\oplus C)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\oplus C)\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(B\otimes A)\oplus(C\otimes A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes B)\oplus(A\otimes C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\oplus s}

(34)

then the following diagram commutes for all objects
A,B,C,D
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\oplus B)\otimes(C\oplus D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes(C\oplus D))\oplus(B\otimes(C\oplus D))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((A\oplus B)\otimes C)\oplus((A\oplus B)\otimes D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes C)\oplus(A\otimes D)\oplus(B\otimes C)\oplus(B\otimes D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes C)\oplus(B\otimes C)\oplus(A\otimes D)\oplus(B\otimes D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta\oplus\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\oplus\gamma\oplus\mathrm{id}}

(35)

A
strict bipermutative functor
is a functor which is a strict symmetric
monoidal functor with respect to both symmetric monoidal structures
(C,\otimes,I)
and
(C,\oplus,O)
.
